The mechanism works through three channels: (i) automatic recognition of foreign main proceedings, which eliminates the need for separate court petitions; (ii) cooperation protocols that allow courts to communicate directly and share assets; (iii) access to financing for debtors in possession, which preserves going‑concern value. For emerging markets, the adoption signal improves the perceived enforceability of cross‑border claims, lowering the risk premium on external commercial borrowings. India’s 2021 amendment introduced provisions for reciprocal recognition with Model‑Law jurisdictions and empowered the National Company Law Tribunal to cooperate with foreign courts. Early cases indicate improved recovery prospects for foreign creditors compared with the pre‑Model‑Law regime. In answer writing, cite the 1997 adoption date, the number of adopting states, the key provisions (recognition, cooperation, financing), the qualitative impact on recovery rates and resolution times, and India’s specific legislative steps.

Central Bank Digital Currency Cross‑Border Projects: mBridge, Project Dunbar and Reserve Currency Implications

Two flagship multi‑CBDC experiments are reshaping the architecture of cross‑border payments. The mBridge project, launched in 2021 by the BIS Innovation Hub Hong Kong together with the Hong Kong Monetary Authority, the Bank of Thailand, the People’s Bank of China and the Central Bank of the United Arab Emirates, uses a permissioned distributed‑ledger platform to enable real‑time, atomic settlement of wholesale payments in multiple CBDCs. Pilot results reported by the BIS indicate a reduction in settlement time from T+2 to seconds and a significant cut in correspondent‑bank fees. Project Dunbar, initiated in 2022 by the BIS Innovation Hub Singapore with the Reserve Bank of Australia, the Bank of Canada, the Monetary Authority of Singapore and the South African Reserve Bank, explores a shared platform supporting multiple CBDCs with a common technical standard (ISO 20022) and a governance model that preserves each central bank’s monetary sovereignty. Both projects address the “trilemma” of cross‑border payments: speed, cost, and transparency. If scaled, multi‑CBDC corridors could reduce reliance on US‑dollar‑denominated correspondent banking, potentially affecting global reserve demand over the long term. However, critical hurdles remain: legal finality of settlement across jurisdictions, anti‑money‑laundering harmonisation, and the willingness of major reserve‑currency issuers (Federal Reserve, ECB) to participate. For exam purposes, recall the participating central banks, the settlement‑time improvement, the fee reduction estimate, the ISO 20022 standard, and the strategic implication for dollar dominance.

  • Confusing the RST’s concessional terms with the IMF’s standard lending facilities; the RST has a 20‑year maturity and 10‑year grace, unlike the 4‑10 year maturities of Stand‑By Arrangements.
  • Assuming import and export pass‑through are symmetric; empirical evidence shows import ERPT roughly three times export ERPT in emerging markets.
  • Attributing all regional safety‑net financing to the CMIM; the ADB’s Countercyclical Support Facility operates independently and pre‑dates the CMIM’s 2014 expansion.
  • Believing the UNCITRAL Model Law automatically applies in all signatory states; domestic enactment is required, and the scope of recognition varies by jurisdiction.
  • Overstating the immediacy of multi‑CBDC displacement of the dollar; current pilots are wholesale‑only, limited in value, and lack participation from the Federal Reserve and ECB.
  • Neglecting the role of invoice‑currency composition when estimating pass‑through; a 10 % depreciation does not translate into a 10 % import‑price rise if a large share of imports are invoiced in the domestic currency.
  • Treating the ADB’s Asian Bond Fund as a reserve‑currency substitute; ABF deepens local‑currency markets but does not create a new reserve asset.
